<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg/220px-Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg/330px-Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg/440px-Protected_area_%22El_Palmar_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="1840" /></a><figcaption>El Palmar Nature Preserve, in northern Chuquisaca</figcaption></figure>
<p>While Bolivia's administrative divisions have similar status under governmental jurisprudence, each department varies in quantitative and qualitative factors. Generally speaking, Departments can be grouped either by geography or by political-cultural orientation. For example, Santa Cruz, Beni and Pando make up the low-lying "Camba" heartlands of the Amazon, Moxos and Chiquitanía. When considering political orientation, Beni, Pando, Santa Cruz, Tarija are generally grouped for regionalist autonomy movements; this region is known as the "Media Luna". Conversely, La Paz, Oruro, Potosí, Cochabamba have been traditionally associated with Andean politics and culture. Today, Chuquisaca vacillates between the Andean cultural bloc and the Camba bloc.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Bolivia" title="Economy of Bolivia">Economy of Bolivia</a></div>
<p>Driven largely by its natural resources Bolivia has become a region leader in measures of economic growth, fiscal stability and foreign reserves,<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> although it remains a historically poor country. Bolivia's estimated 2012 gross domestic product (GDP) totaled $27.43&#160;billion at official exchange rate and $56.14&#160;billion at purchasing power parity. Despite a series of mostly political setbacks, between 2006 and 2009 the Morales administration spurred growth higher than at any point in the preceding 30 years. The growth was accompanied by a moderate decrease in inequality.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Under Morales, per capita GDP doubled from US$1,182 in 2006 to US$2,238 in 2012. GDP growth under Morales averaged 5 percent a year, and in 2014 only <a href="/wiki/Panama" title="Panama">Panama</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic" title="Dominican Republic">Dominican Republic</a> performed better in all of Latin America.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Bolivia's nominal GDP increased from 11.5&#160;billion in 2006 to 41&#160;billion in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolivia in 2016 boasted the highest proportional rate of financial reserves of any nation in the world, with Bolivia's rainy day fund totaling some US$15&#160;billion or nearly two-thirds of total annual GDP, up from a fifth of GDP in 2005. Even the IMF was impressed by Morales' fiscal prudence.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Agriculture">Agriculture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Agriculture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Agriculture is less relevant in the country's GDP compared to the rest of Latin America. The country produces close to 10 million tons of <a href="/wiki/Sugarcane" title="Sugarcane">sugarcane</a> per year and is the 10th largest producer of <a href="/wiki/Soybean" title="Soybean">soybean</a> in the world. It also has considerable yields of maize, potato, <a href="/wiki/Sorghum" title="Sorghum">sorghum</a>, banana, rice, and wheat. The country's largest exports are based on soy (soybean meal and <a href="/wiki/Soybean_oil" title="Soybean oil">soybean oil</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> The culture of <a href="/wiki/Soy" class="mw-redirect" title="Soy">soy</a> was brought by Brazilians to the country: in 2006, almost 50% of soy producers in Bolivia were people from Brazil, or descendants of Brazilians. The first Brazilian producers began to arrive in the country in the 1990s. Before that, there was a lot of land in the country that was not used, or where only subsistence agriculture was practiced.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolivia's most lucrative agricultural product continues to be <a href="/wiki/Coca" title="Coca">coca</a>, of which Bolivia is currently the world's third largest cultivator.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mineral_resources">Mineral resources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Mineral resources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cerro_ricco.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cerro_ricco.jpg/220px-Cerro_ricco.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cerro_ricco.jpg/330px-Cerro_ricco.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Cerro_ricco.jpg/440px-Cerro_ricco.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1800" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Cerro_Rico" title="Cerro Rico">Cerro Rico</a> in <a href="/wiki/Potos%C3%AD" title="Potosí">Potosí</a>, still an important mining site since the colonial times.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bolivia, while historically renowned for its vast mineral wealth, is relatively under-explored in geological and mineralogical terms. The country is rich in various mineral and natural resources, sitting at the heart of South America in the Central Andes.
</p><p>Mining is a major sector of the economy, with most of the country's exports being dependent on it.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> In 2019, the country was the eighth largest world producer of silver;<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> fifth largest world producer of <a href="/wiki/Tin" title="Tin">tin</a><sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Antimony" title="Antimony">antimony</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> seventh largest producer of <a href="/wiki/Zinc" title="Zinc">zinc</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> 
eighth largest producer of lead,<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> fourth largest world producer of <a href="/wiki/Boron" title="Boron">boron</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> and the sixth largest world producer of <a href="/wiki/Tungsten" title="Tungsten">tungsten</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> The country also has considerable gold production, which varies close to 25 tons/year, and also has <a href="/wiki/Amethyst" title="Amethyst">amethyst</a> extraction.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolivia has the world's largest lithium reserves, second largest antimony reserves, third largest iron ore reserves, sixth largest tin reserves, ninth largest lead, silver, and copper reserves, tenth largest zinc reserves, and undisclosed but productive reserves of gold and tungsten. Additionally, there is believed to be considerable reserves of uranium and nickel present in the country's largely under-explored eastern regions. Diamond reserves may also be present in some formations of the Serranías Chiquitanas in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Department_(Bolivia)" title="Santa Cruz Department (Bolivia)">Santa Cruz Department</a>.
</p><p>Bolivia has the second largest natural gas reserves in South America.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Its natural gas exports bring in millions of dollars per day, in royalties, rents, and taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> From 2007 to 2017, what is referred to as the "government take" on gas totaled approximately $22&#160;billion.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government held a binding referendum in 2005 on the Hydrocarbon Law. Among other provisions, the law requires that companies sell their production to the state hydrocarbons company <a href="/wiki/YPFB" title="YPFB">Yacimientos Petroliferos Fiscales Bolivianos</a> (YPFB) and for domestic demand to be met before exporting hydrocarbons and increased the state's royalties from natural gas.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> The passage of the Hydrocarbon law in opposition to then-President <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Mesa" title="Carlos Mesa">Carlos Mesa</a> can be understood as part of the <a href="/wiki/Bolivian_gas_conflict" title="Bolivian gas conflict">Bolivian gas conflict</a> which ultimately resulted in election of <a href="/wiki/Evo_Morales" title="Evo Morales">Evo Morales</a>, Bolivia's first indigenous president.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat,_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg/220px-Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="123" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg/330px-Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg/440px-Lithium_mine_at_Bolivia%C2%B4s_Uyuni_Salt_Flat%2C_on_a_CBERS4_MUX_yesterday%C2%B4s_image.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3777" data-file-height="2114" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Lithium" title="Lithium">Lithium</a> mine in the <a href="/wiki/Salar_de_Uyuni" title="Salar de Uyuni">Salar de Uyuni</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/United_States_Geological_Survey" title="United States Geological Survey">US Geological Service</a> estimates that Bolivia has 21 million tonnes of <a href="/wiki/Lithium" title="Lithium">lithium</a>, which represent at least 25% of world reserves – the largest in the world. However, to mine for it would involve disturbing the country's salt flats (called <a href="/wiki/Salar_de_Uyuni" title="Salar de Uyuni">Salar de Uyuni</a>), an important natural feature which boosts tourism in the region. The government does not want to destroy this unique natural landscape to meet the rising world demand for lithium.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, sustainable extraction of lithium is attempted by the government. This project is carried out by the public company "Recursos Evaporíticos" subsidiary of COMIBOL.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism">Tourism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Tourism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Bolivia" title="Tourism in Bolivia">Tourism in Bolivia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vale_9.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Vale_9.jpg/220px-Vale_9.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Vale_9.jpg/330px-Vale_9.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Vale_9.jpg/440px-Vale_9.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>Waterfall in the <a href="/wiki/Torotoro_National_Park" title="Torotoro National Park">Torotoro National Park</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The income from tourism has become increasingly important. <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Bolivia" title="Tourism in Bolivia">Bolivia's tourist industry</a> has placed an emphasis on attracting ethnic diversity.<sup id="cite_ref-Xie2011_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Xie2011-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> The most visited places include <a href="/wiki/Nevado_Sajama" title="Nevado Sajama">Nevado Sajama</a>, <a href="/wiki/Torotoro_National_Park" title="Torotoro National Park">Torotoro National Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madidi_National_Park" title="Madidi National Park">Madidi National Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tiwanaku" title="Tiwanaku">Tiwanaku</a> and the city of <a href="/wiki/La_Paz" title="La Paz">La Paz</a>.
</p><p>The best known of the various festivals found in the country is the "<a href="/wiki/Carnaval_de_Oruro" title="Carnaval de Oruro">Carnaval de Oruro</a>", which was among the first 19 "<a href="/wiki/Masterpieces_of_the_Oral_and_Intangible_Heritage_of_Humanity" title="Masterpieces of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity">Masterpieces of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity</a>", as proclaimed by UNESCO in May 2001.<sup id="cite_ref-protection_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-protection-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Bolivia" title="Transport in Bolivia">Transport in Bolivia</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Roads">Roads</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Roads">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bolivia's <a href="/wiki/Yungas_Road" title="Yungas Road">Yungas Road</a> was called the "world's most dangerous road" by the <a href="/wiki/Inter-American_Development_Bank" title="Inter-American Development Bank">Inter-American Development Bank</a>, called (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">El Camino de la Muerte</i></span>) in Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The northern portion of the road, much of it unpaved and without guardrails, was cut into the <a href="/wiki/Cordillera_Oriental_(Bolivia)" title="Cordillera Oriental (Bolivia)">Cordillera Oriental Mountain</a> in the 1930s. The fall from the narrow 12 feet (3.7&#160;m) path is as much as 2,000 feet (610&#160;m) in some places and due to the humid weather from the <a href="/wiki/Amazon_rainforest" title="Amazon rainforest">Amazon</a> there are often poor conditions like mudslides and falling rocks.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Each year over 25,000 bikers cycle along the 40 miles (64&#160;km) road. In 2018, an Israeli woman was killed by a falling rock while cycling on the road.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Apolo,_La_Paz" title="Apolo, La Paz">Apolo</a> road goes deep into <a href="/wiki/La_Paz" title="La Paz">La Paz</a>. Roads in this area were originally built to allow access to mines located near <a href="/wiki/Charazani" title="Charazani">Charazani</a>. Other noteworthy roads run to <a href="/wiki/Coroico" title="Coroico">Coroico</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sorata" title="Sorata">Sorata</a>, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Zongo_Valley&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Zongo Valley (page does not exist)">Zongo Valley</a> (<a href="/wiki/Illimani" title="Illimani">Illimani</a> mountain), and along the <a href="/wiki/Cochabamba_Department" title="Cochabamba Department">Cochabamba</a> highway (<span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es">carretera</i></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> According to researchers with the <a href="/wiki/Center_for_International_Forestry_Research" title="Center for International Forestry Research">Center for International Forestry Research</a> (CIFOR), Bolivia's road network was still underdeveloped as of 2014. In lowland areas of Bolivia there is less than 2,000 kilometers (2,000,000&#160;m) of paved road. There have been some recent investments; <a href="/wiki/Animal_husbandry" title="Animal husbandry">animal husbandry</a> has expanded in <a href="/wiki/Guayaramer%C3%ADn" title="Guayaramerín">Guayaramerín</a>, which might be due to a new road connecting Guayaramerín with <a href="/wiki/Trinidad,_Bolivia" title="Trinidad, Bolivia">Trinidad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The country only opened its first <a href="/wiki/Dual_carriageway" title="Dual carriageway">duplicated highway</a> in 2015: a 203&#160;km stretch between the capital La Paz and Oruro.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Air">Air</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Air">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_airlines_of_Bolivia" title="List of airlines of Bolivia">List of airlines of Bolivia</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Bolivia" title="List of airports in Bolivia">List of airports in Bolivia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg/220px-Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg/330px-Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg/440px-Aviones_de_BoA_en_Cochabamba.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Boliviana_de_Aviaci%C3%B3n" title="Boliviana de Aviación">Boliviana de Aviación</a> (BoA) is a state-owned company and the country's largest airline. Two BoA Boeing 737-300s parked at <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Wilstermann_International_Airport" title="Jorge Wilstermann International Airport">Jorge Wilstermann International Airport</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The General Directorate of Civil Aeronautics (Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil—DGAC) formerly part of the FAB, administers a civil aeronautics school called the National Institute of Civil Aeronautics (Instituto Nacional de Aeronáutica Civil—INAC), and two commercial air transport services TAM and TAB.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/TAM_%E2%80%93_Transporte_A%C3%A9reo_Militar" title="TAM – Transporte Aéreo Militar">TAM – Transporte Aéreo Militar</a> (the Bolivian Military Airline) was an airline based in La Paz, Bolivia. It was the civilian wing of the 'Fuerza Aérea Boliviana' (the Bolivian Air Force), operating passenger services to remote towns and communities in the North and Northeast of Bolivia. TAM (a.k.a. TAM Group 71) has been a part of the FAB since 1945. The airline company has suspended its operations since 23 September 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Boliviana_de_Aviaci%C3%B3n" title="Boliviana de Aviación">Boliviana de Aviación</a>, often referred to as simply BoA, is the flag carrier airline of Bolivia and is wholly owned by the country's government.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A private airline serving regional destinations is <a href="/wiki/L%C3%ADnea_A%C3%A9rea_Amaszonas" title="Línea Aérea Amaszonas">Línea Aérea Amaszonas</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> with services including some international destinations.
</p><p>Although a civil transport airline, <a href="/wiki/TAB_%E2%80%93_Transportes_A%C3%A9reos_Bolivianos" class="mw-redirect" title="TAB – Transportes Aéreos Bolivianos">TAB – Transportes Aéreos Bolivianos</a>, was created as a subsidiary company of the FAB in 1977. It is subordinate to the Air Transport Management (Gerencia de Transportes Aéreos) and is headed by an FAB general. TAB, a charter heavy cargo airline, links Bolivia with most countries of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a>; its inventory includes a fleet of Hercules C130 aircraft. TAB is headquartered adjacent to <a href="/wiki/El_Alto_International_Airport" title="El Alto International Airport">El Alto International Airport</a>. TAB flies to Miami and <a href="/wiki/Houston" title="Houston">Houston</a>, with a stop in <a href="/wiki/Panama" title="Panama">Panama</a>.
</p><p>The three largest, and main international airports in Bolivia are <a href="/wiki/El_Alto_International_Airport" title="El Alto International Airport">El Alto International Airport</a> in La Paz, <a href="/wiki/Viru_Viru_International_Airport" title="Viru Viru International Airport">Viru Viru International Airport</a> in Santa Cruz, and <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Wilstermann_International_Airport" title="Jorge Wilstermann International Airport">Jorge Wilstermann International Airport</a> in Cochabamba. There are regional airports in other cities that connect to these three hubs.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Technology">Technology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Technology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bolivia owns a <a href="/wiki/Communications_satellite" title="Communications satellite">communications satellite</a> which was <a href="/wiki/Offshore_company" title="Offshore company">offshored/outsourced</a> and launched by China, named <a href="/wiki/T%C3%BApac_Katari_1" title="Túpac Katari 1">Túpac Katari 1</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, it was announced that electrical power advancements include a planned $300&#160;million nuclear reactor developed by the Russian nuclear company <a href="/wiki/Rosatom" title="Rosatom">Rosatom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Bolivia was ranked 104th in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021, up from 110th in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Water_supply_and_sanitation">Water supply and sanitation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Water supply and sanitation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Bolivia" title="Water supply and sanitation in Bolivia">Water supply and sanitation in Bolivia</a></div>
<p>Bolivia's drinking water and <a href="/wiki/Sanitation" title="Sanitation">sanitation</a> coverage has greatly improved since 1990 due to a considerable increase in sectoral investment. However, the country has the continent's lowest coverage levels and services are of low quality. Political and institutional instability have contributed to the weakening of the sector's institutions at the national and local levels.
</p><p>Two concessions to foreign private companies in two of the three largest cities – <a href="/wiki/Cochabamba" title="Cochabamba">Cochabamba</a> and <a href="/wiki/La_Paz" title="La Paz">La Paz</a>/<a href="/wiki/El_Alto" title="El Alto">El Alto</a> – were prematurely ended in 2000 and 2006 respectively. The country's second largest city, <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_de_la_Sierra" title="Santa Cruz de la Sierra">Santa Cruz de la Sierra</a>, manages its own water and sanitation system relatively successfully by way of cooperatives. The government of <a href="/wiki/Evo_Morales" title="Evo Morales">Evo Morales</a> intends to strengthen citizen participation within the sector. Increasing coverage requires a substantial increase of investment financing.
</p><p>According to the government the main problems in the sector are low access to sanitation throughout the country; low access to water in rural areas; insufficient and ineffective investments; a low visibility of community service providers; a lack of respect of indigenous customs; "technical and institutional difficulties in the design and implementation of projects"; a lack of capacity to operate and maintain infrastructure; an institutional framework that is "not consistent with the political change in the country"; "ambiguities in the social participation schemes"; a reduction in the quantity and 
quality of water due to climate change; pollution and a lack of integrated water resources management; and the lack of policies and programs for the reuse of wastewater.<sup id="cite_ref-PNSB_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PNSB-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Only 27% of the population has access to <a href="/wiki/Improved_sanitation" title="Improved sanitation">improved sanitation</a>, 80 to 88% has access to <a href="/wiki/Improved_water_source" title="Improved water source">improved water sources</a>. Coverage in urban areas is bigger than in rural ones.<sup id="cite_ref-JMP_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JMP-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some regions of Bolivia are largely under the power of the <i>ganaderos</i>, the large cattle and pig owners, and many small farmers are still reduced to peons. Nevertheless, the presence of the state has been clearly reinforced under the government of <a href="/wiki/Evo_Morales" title="Evo Morales">Evo Morales</a>. The government tends to accommodate the interests of large landowners while trying to improve the living and working conditions of small farmers.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Agriculture_2">Agriculture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Agriculture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_Bolivia" title="Agriculture in Bolivia">Agriculture in Bolivia</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Bolivian_cuisine" title="Bolivian cuisine">Bolivian cuisine</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Bolivian_wine" title="Bolivian wine">Bolivian wine</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya,_Bolivia,_Lake_Titicaca.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG/220px-Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG/330px-Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG/440px-Chenopodium_quinoa_in_Cachilaya%2C_Bolivia%2C_Lake_Titicaca.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Quinoa" title="Quinoa">Quinoa</a> field near <a href="/wiki/Lake_Titicaca" title="Lake Titicaca">Lake Titicaca</a>. Bolivia is the world's second largest producer of the crop.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The agrarian reform promised by Evo Morales – and approved in a referendum by nearly 80 per cent of the population – has never been implemented. Intended to abolish <a href="/wiki/Latifundium" title="Latifundium">latifundism</a> by reducing the maximum size of properties that do not have an "economic and social function" to 5,000 hectares, with the remainder to be distributed among small agricultural workers and landless indigenous people, it was strongly opposed by the Bolivian oligarchy. In 2009, the government gave in to the agribusiness sector, which in return committed to end the pressure it was exerting and jeopardizing until the new constitution was in place.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, a series of economic reforms and projects have improved the condition of modest peasant families. They received farm machinery, tractors, fertilizers, seeds and breeding stock, while the state built irrigation systems, roads and bridges to make it easier for them to sell their produce in the markets. The situation of many indigenous people and small farmers was regularized through the granting of land titles for the land they were using.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2007, the government created a "Bank for Productive Development" through which small workers and agricultural producers can borrow easily, at low rates and with repayment terms adapted to agricultural cycles. As a result of improved banking supervision, borrowing rates have been reduced by a factor of three between 2014 and 2019 across all banking institutions for small and medium-sized agricultural producers. In addition, the law now requires banks to devote at least 60% of their resources to productive credits or to the construction of social housing.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the creation of the Food Production Support Enterprise (Emapa), the government sought to stabilize the domestic market for agricultural products by buying the best prices for the production of small and medium-sized farmers, thus forcing agribusinesses to offer them fairer remuneration. According to Vice President <a href="/wiki/%C3%81lvaro_Garc%C3%ADa_Linera" title="Álvaro García Linera">Àlvaro García Linera</a>, "by setting the rules of the game, the State establishes a new balance of power that gives more power to small producers. Wealth is better redistributed to balance the power of the agribusiness sector. This generates stability, which allows the economy to flourish and benefits everyone.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_172-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Bolivia" title="Demographics of Bolivia">Demographics of Bolivia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG/220px-Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG/330px-Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG/440px-Centro_de_La_Paz_en_Bolivia.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption>People in La Paz city center</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to the last two censuses carried out by the Bolivian National Statistics Institute <i>(Instituto Nacional de Estadística</i>, INE), the population increased from 8,274,325 (from which 4,123,850 were men and 4,150,475 were women) in 2001 to 10,059,856 in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-Cen2012_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cen2012-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the last fifty years the Bolivian population has tripled, reaching a population growth rate of 2.25%. The growth of the population in the inter-census periods (1950–1976 and 1976–1992) was approximately 2.05%, while between the last period, 1992–2001, it reached 2.74% annually.
</p><p>Some 67.49% of Bolivians live in urban areas, while the remaining 32.51% in rural areas. The most part of the population (70%) is concentrated in the departments of <a href="/wiki/La_Paz_Department_(Bolivia)" title="La Paz Department (Bolivia)">La Paz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Department_(Bolivia)" title="Santa Cruz Department (Bolivia)">Santa Cruz</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cochabamba_Department" title="Cochabamba Department">Cochabamba</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/Andes" title="Andes">Andean</a> <a href="/wiki/Altiplano" title="Altiplano">Altiplano</a> region the departments of La Paz and <a href="/wiki/Oruro_Department" title="Oruro Department">Oruro</a> hold the largest percentage of population, in the valley region the largest percentage is held by the departments of Cochabamba and <a href="/wiki/Chuquisaca_Department" title="Chuquisaca Department">Chuquisaca</a>, while in the Llanos region by Santa Cruz and <a href="/wiki/Beni_Department" title="Beni Department">Beni</a>. At national level, the population density is 8.49, with variations marked between 0.8 (<a href="/wiki/Pando_Department" title="Pando Department">Pando Department</a>) and 26.2 (Cochabamba Department).
</p><p>The largest population center is located in the so-called "central axis" and in the Llanos region. Bolivia has a young population. According to the 2011 census, 59% of the population is between 15 and 59 years old, 39% is less than 15 years old. Almost 60% of the population is younger than 25 years of age.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Ethnic groups">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Macheteros.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Macheteros.JPG/220px-Macheteros.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Macheteros.JPG/330px-Macheteros.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Macheteros.JPG/440px-Macheteros.JPG 2x" data-file-width="687" data-file-height="514" /></a><figcaption><i>Danza de los macheteros</i>, typical dance from <a href="/wiki/San_Ignacio_de_Moxos" title="San Ignacio de Moxos">San Ignacio de Moxos</a>, Bolivia</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Flickr_-_archer10_(Dennis)_-_Bolivia-133.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg/170px-Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="254" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg/255px-Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg/340px-Flickr_-_archer10_%28Dennis%29_-_Bolivia-133.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4416" data-file-height="6592" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Aymara_people" title="Aymara people">Aymara</a> man, near Lake Titicaca, Bolivia</figcaption></figure>
<p>The vast majority of Bolivians are <a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">mestizo</a> (with the indigenous component higher than the European one), although the government has not included the cultural self-identification "mestizo" in the November 2012 census.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> There are approximately three dozen native groups totaling approximately half of the Bolivian population – the largest proportion of indigenous people in the Americas. A 2018 estimate of racial classification put mestizo (mixed white and Amerindian) at 68%, indigenous at 20%, white at 5%, <a href="/wiki/Cholo#Bolivia" title="Cholo">cholo</a> at 2%, black at 1%, other at 4%, while 2% were unspecified; 44% attributed themselves to some indigenous group, predominantly the linguistic categories of <a href="/wiki/Quechuas" class="mw-redirect" title="Quechuas">Quechuas</a> or <a href="/wiki/Aymara_people" title="Aymara people">Aymaras</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/White_Latin_Americans" title="White Latin Americans">White Bolivians</a> comprised about 14% of the population in 2006, and are usually concentrated in the largest cities: <a href="/wiki/La_Paz" title="La Paz">La Paz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_de_la_Sierra" title="Santa Cruz de la Sierra">Santa Cruz de la Sierra</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cochabamba" title="Cochabamba">Cochabamba</a>, but as well in some minor cities like <a href="/wiki/Tarija" title="Tarija">Tarija</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sucre" title="Sucre">Sucre</a>. The ancestry of whites and the white ancestry of mestizos lies within Europe and the Middle East, most notably Spain, Italy, Germany, <a href="/wiki/Croatia" title="Croatia">Croatia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Department,_Bolivia" class="mw-redirect" title="Santa Cruz Department, Bolivia">Santa Cruz Department</a>, there are several dozen colonies of German-speaking <a href="/wiki/Russian_Mennonite" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Mennonite">Mennonites from Russia</a> totaling around 40,000 inhabitants (as of 2012<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>).<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Afro-Bolivian" class="mw-redirect" title="Afro-Bolivian">Afro-Bolivians</a>, descendants of <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">African slaves</a> who arrived in the time of the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a>, inhabit the <a href="/wiki/La_Paz_Department,_Bolivia" class="mw-redirect" title="La Paz Department, Bolivia">department of La Paz</a>, and are located mainly in the provinces of <a href="/wiki/Nor_Yungas_Province" title="Nor Yungas Province">Nor Yungas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sud_Yungas_Province" title="Sud Yungas Province">Sud Yungas</a>. Slavery was abolished in Bolivia in 1831.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> There are also important communities of <a href="/wiki/Japanese_Bolivians" class="mw-redirect" title="Japanese Bolivians">Japanese</a> (14,000<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>) and Lebanese (12,900<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>).
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">Indigenous peoples</a>, also called <i>"originarios"</i> ("native" or "original") and less frequently, <i>Amerindians</i>, could be categorized by geographic area, such as <a href="/wiki/Andes" title="Andes">Andean</a>, like the <a href="/wiki/Aymara_people" title="Aymara people">Aymaras</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quechua_people" title="Quechua people">Quechuas</a> (who formed the ancient <a href="/wiki/Inca_Empire" title="Inca Empire">Inca Empire</a>), who are concentrated in the western departments of <a href="/wiki/La_Paz_Department,_Bolivia" class="mw-redirect" title="La Paz Department, Bolivia">La Paz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Potos%C3%AD_Department" title="Potosí Department">Potosí</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oruro_Department" title="Oruro Department">Oruro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cochabamba_Department" title="Cochabamba Department">Cochabamba</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chuquisaca_Department" title="Chuquisaca Department">Chuquisaca</a>. There also are ethnic populations in the east, composed of the <a href="/wiki/Chiquitano" title="Chiquitano">Chiquitano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chan%C3%A9" title="Chané">Chané</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guaran%C3%AD_people" title="Guaraní people">Guaraní</a> and <a href="/wiki/Moxo_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Moxo people">Moxos</a>, among others, who inhabit the departments of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Department,_Bolivia" class="mw-redirect" title="Santa Cruz Department, Bolivia">Santa Cruz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Beni_Department" title="Beni Department">Beni</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tarija_Department" title="Tarija Department">Tarija</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pando_Department" title="Pando Department">Pando</a>.
</p><p>There are small numbers of European citizens from Germany, France, Italy and Portugal, as well as from other countries of the Americas, as Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Cuba, Ecuador, the United States, Paraguay, Peru, Mexico and Venezuela, among others. There are important Peruvian colonies in <a href="/wiki/La_Paz" title="La Paz">La Paz</a>, <a href="/wiki/El_Alto" title="El Alto">El Alto</a> and <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_de_la_Sierra" title="Santa Cruz de la Sierra">Santa Cruz de la Sierra</a>.
</p><p>There are around 140,000 <a href="/wiki/Mennonites_in_Bolivia" title="Mennonites in Bolivia">Mennonites in Bolivia</a> of Friesian, Flemish and German ethnic origins.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Language">Language</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Language">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Bolivia" title="Languages of Bolivia">Languages of Bolivia</a></div>
<p>Bolivia has great linguistic diversity as a result of its <a href="/wiki/Multiculturalism" title="Multiculturalism">multiculturalism</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Bolivia" title="Constitution of Bolivia">Constitution of Bolivia</a> recognizes 36 official languages besides Spanish: <a href="/wiki/Aymara_language" title="Aymara language">Aymara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Araona_language" title="Araona language">Araona</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baure_language" title="Baure language">Baure</a>, <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9siro_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Bésiro language">Bésiro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canichana_language" title="Canichana language">Canichana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cavine%C3%B1o_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Cavineño language">Cavineño</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cayubaba_language" title="Cayubaba language">Cayubaba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A1cobo_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Chácobo language">Chácobo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chim%C3%A1n_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Chimán language">Chimán</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ese_Ejja_language" title="Ese Ejja language">Ese Ejja</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guaran%C3%AD_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Guaraní language">Guaraní</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guarasuawe_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Guarasuawe language">Guarasu'we</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guarayu_language" title="Guarayu language">Guarayu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Itonama_language" title="Itonama language">Itonama</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leco_language" title="Leco language">Leco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Machajuyai-Kallawaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Machajuyai-Kallawaya">Machajuyai-Kallawaya</a>, <a href="/wiki/Machineri_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Machineri language">Machineri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maropa_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Maropa language">Maropa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moje%C3%B1o-Ignaciano" class="mw-redirect" title="Mojeño-Ignaciano">Mojeño-Ignaciano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moje%C3%B1o-Trinitario" class="mw-redirect" title="Mojeño-Trinitario">Mojeño-Trinitario</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mor%C3%A9_language_(Bolivia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Moré language (Bolivia)">Moré</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moset%C3%A9n_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Mosetén language">Mosetén</a>, <a href="/wiki/Movima_language" title="Movima language">Movima</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pacawara_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Pacawara language">Pacawara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Puquina_language" title="Puquina language">Puquina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quechua_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Quechua language">Quechua</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sirion%C3%B3_language" title="Sirionó language">Sirionó</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tacana_language" title="Tacana language">Tacana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tapiet%C3%A9_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Tapieté language">Tapieté</a>, <a href="/wiki/Toromona_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Toromona language">Toromona</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uru-Chipaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Uru-Chipaya">Uru-Chipaya</a>, <a href="/wiki/Weenhayek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Weenhayek language">Weenhayek</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yaminawa_language" title="Yaminawa language">Yaminawa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yuki_language_(Bolivia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Yuki language (Bolivia)">Yuki</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yuracar%C3%A9_language" title="Yuracaré language">Yuracaré</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Zamuco_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Zamuco language">Zamuco</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-languages_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-languages-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_(37270469644).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg/170px-20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg/255px-20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg/340px-20170807_Bolivia_1373_crop_Potos%C3%AD_sRGB_%2837270469644%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2700" data-file-height="3599" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Church_of_San_Lorenzo_de_Carangas" title="Church of San Lorenzo de Carangas">Church of San Lorenzo de Carangas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Potos%C3%AD" title="Potosí">Potosí</a>, mid-16th century–1744.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Spanish is the most spoken official language in the country, according to the 2001 census; as it is spoken by two-thirds of the population. All legal and official documents issued by the State, including the Constitution, the main private and public institutions, the media, and commercial activities, are in Spanish.
</p><p>The main indigenous languages are: <a href="/wiki/Quechua_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Quechua language">Quechua</a> (21.2% of the population in the 2001 census), <a href="/wiki/Aymara_language" title="Aymara language">Aymara</a> (14.6%), <a href="/wiki/Guarani_language" title="Guarani language">Guarani</a> (0.6%) and others (0.4%) including the <a href="/wiki/Moxos_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Moxos language">Moxos</a> in the department of Beni.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Plautdietsch" title="Plautdietsch">Plautdietsch</a>, a <a href="/wiki/German_dialects" title="German dialects">German dialect</a>, is spoken by about 70,000 <a href="/wiki/Mennonites_in_Bolivia" title="Mennonites in Bolivia">Mennonites</a> in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Department_(Bolivia)" title="Santa Cruz Department (Bolivia)">Santa Cruz</a>. <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a> is spoken mainly in the areas close to Brazil.
</p><p>Bilingual education was implemented in Bolivia under the leadership of <a href="/wiki/Evo_Morales" title="Evo Morales">President Evo Morales</a>. His program placed emphasis on the expansion of indigenous languages in the educational systems of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Bolivia" title="Religion in Bolivia">Religion in Bolivia</a></div>

</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:Purple; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a> (77%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:Blue; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a> (16%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:grey; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Other (3%)</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:White; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Irreligion" title="Irreligion">No religion</a> (4%)</div>
</div>
</div></div>
<p>Bolivia is a constitutionally <a href="/wiki/Secular_state" title="Secular state">secular state</a> that guarantees the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_religion" title="Freedom of religion">freedom of religion</a> and the independence of government from religion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConstitute_Assembly_of_Bolivia20072_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitute_Assembly_of_Bolivia20072-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2001 census conducted by the <a href="/wiki/National_Institute_of_Statistics_of_Bolivia" title="National Institute of Statistics of Bolivia">National Institute of Statistics of Bolivia</a>, 78% of the population is <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Roman Catholic</a>, followed by 19% that are <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a>, as well as a small number of Bolivians that are <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a>, and 3% non-religious.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives" title="Association of Religion Data Archives">Association of Religion Data Archives</a> (relying on the World Christian Database) records that in 2010, 92.5% of Bolivians identified as Christian (of any denomination), 3.1% identified with indigenous religion, 2.2% identified as <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí</a>, 1.9% identified as agnostic, and all other groups constituted 0.1% or less.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Much of the indigenous population adheres to different traditional beliefs marked by <a href="/wiki/Inculturation" title="Inculturation">inculturation</a> or <a href="/wiki/Syncretic" class="mw-redirect" title="Syncretic">syncretism</a> with Christianity. The cult of <a href="/wiki/Pachamama" title="Pachamama">Pachamama</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> or "Mother Earth", is notable. The veneration of the <a href="/wiki/Virgen_de_Copacabana" title="Virgen de Copacabana">Virgin of Copacabana</a>, Virgin of Urkupiña and Virgin of Socavón, is also an important feature of <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">Christian pilgrimage</a>. There also are important <a href="/wiki/Aymara_people" title="Aymara people">Aymaran communities</a> near <a href="/wiki/Lake_Titicaca" title="Lake Titicaca">Lake Titicaca</a> that have a strong devotion to <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Zebedee" class="mw-redirect" title="James, son of Zebedee">James the Apostle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Deities worshiped in Bolivia include <a href="/wiki/Ekeko" title="Ekeko">Ekeko</a>, the Aymaran god of abundance and prosperity, whose day is celebrated every 24 January, and <a href="/wiki/Guarani_mythology" title="Guarani mythology">Tupá</a>, a god of the <a href="/wiki/Guaran%C3%AD_people" title="Guaraní people">Guaraní people</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Largest_cities_and_towns">Largest cities and towns</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Largest cities and towns">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_and_largest_towns_in_Bolivia" title="List of cities and largest towns in Bolivia">List of cities and largest towns in Bolivia</a>
</p><p>Approximately 67% of Bolivians live in urban areas,<sup id="cite_ref-factbook_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-factbook-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> among the lowest proportion in South America. Nevertheless, the rate of urbanization is growing steadily, at around 2.5% annually. According to the 2012 census, there are total of 3,158,691 households in Bolivia – an increase of 887,960 from 2001.<sup id="cite_ref-Cen2012_173-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cen2012-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> In 2009, 75.4% of homes were classified as a house, hut, or Pahuichi; 3.3% were apartments; 21.1% were rental residences; and 0.1% were <a href="/wiki/Mobile_home" title="Mobile home">mobile homes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Most of the country's largest cities are located in the highlands of the west and central regions.
</p>

</tbody></table></div><p><sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Bolivia" title="Culture of Bolivia">Culture of Bolivia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Bolivia" title="Music of Bolivia">Music of Bolivia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Bolivia" title="Public holidays in Bolivia">Public holidays in Bolivia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg/220px-Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg/330px-Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg/440px-Ni%C3%B1os_ejecutando_una_tarkeada.jpg 2x" data-file-width="652" data-file-height="489" /></a><figcaption>Bolivian children playing <a href="/wiki/Tarka_(flute)" title="Tarka (flute)">tarka</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Bolivian culture has been heavily influenced by the Spanish, the Aymara, the Quechua, as well as the popular cultures of Latin America as a whole.
</p><p>The cultural development is divided into three distinct periods: precolumbian, colonial, and republican. Important <a href="/wiki/Archaeology" title="Archaeology">archaeological</a> ruins, gold and silver ornaments, stone monuments, <a href="/wiki/Ceramic" title="Ceramic">ceramics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Weaving" title="Weaving">weavings</a> remain from several important pre-Columbian cultures. Major ruins include <a href="/wiki/Tiwanaku" title="Tiwanaku">Tiwanaku</a>, <a href="/wiki/El_Fuerte_de_Samaipata" title="El Fuerte de Samaipata">El Fuerte de Samaipata</a>, <a href="/wiki/Inkallaqta" title="Inkallaqta">Inkallaqta</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iskanwaya" title="Iskanwaya">Iskanwaya</a>. The country abounds in other sites that are difficult to reach and have seen little archaeological exploration.<sup id="cite_ref-BackgroundNote_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BackgroundNote-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg/220px-Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg/330px-Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/ba/Diablada_oruro_fraternidad.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="244" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Diablada_of_Oruro" class="mw-redirect" title="Diablada of Oruro">Diablada</a>, dance primeval, typical and main of <a href="/wiki/Carnival_of_Oruro" class="mw-redirect" title="Carnival of Oruro">Carnival of Oruro</a> a <a href="/wiki/Masterpieces_of_the_Oral_and_Intangible_Heritage_of_Humanity" title="Masterpieces of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity">Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity</a> since 2001 in Bolivia (<i>File: Fraternidad Artística y Cultural "La Diablada"</i>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Spanish brought their own tradition of religious art which, in the hands of local native and mestizo builders and <a href="/wiki/Artisan" title="Artisan">artisans</a>, developed into a rich and distinctive style of architecture, painting, and sculpture known as "Mestizo Baroque". The colonial period produced not only the paintings of Pérez de Holguín, Flores, Bitti, and others but also the works of skilled but unknown stonecutters, woodcarvers, goldsmiths, and silversmiths. An important body of Native Baroque religious music of the colonial period was recovered and has been performed internationally to wide acclaim since 1994.<sup id="cite_ref-BackgroundNote_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BackgroundNote-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolivian artists of stature in the 20th century include <a href="/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Luisa_Pacheco" title="María Luisa Pacheco">María Luisa Pacheco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roberto_Mamani_Mamani" title="Roberto Mamani Mamani">Roberto Mamani Mamani</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alejandro_Mario_Yllanes" title="Alejandro Mario Yllanes">Alejandro Mario Yllanes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alfredo_Da_Silva" title="Alfredo Da Silva">Alfredo Da Silva</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Marina_N%C3%BA%C3%B1ez_del_Prado" title="Marina Núñez del Prado">Marina Núñez del Prado</a>.
</p><p>Bolivia has a rich <a href="/wiki/Folklore" title="Folklore">folklore</a>. Its regional <a href="/wiki/Folk_music" title="Folk music">folk music</a> is distinctive and varied. The "devil dances" at the annual carnival of Oruro are one of the great folkloric events of South America, as is the lesser known carnival at <a href="/wiki/Tarabuco" title="Tarabuco">Tarabuco</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BackgroundNote_195-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BackgroundNote-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Bolivia" title="Education in Bolivia">Education in Bolivia</a></div>
<p>In 2008, following <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> standards, Bolivia was declared free of <a href="/wiki/Literacy" title="Literacy">illiteracy</a>, making it the fourth country in South America to attain this status.<sup id="cite_ref-literacyBBC_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-literacyBBC-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bolivia has public and private universities. Among them: <a href="/wiki/University_of_Saint_Francis_Xavier" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Saint Francis Xavier">Universidad Mayor, Real y Pontificia de San Francisco Xavier de Chuquisaca</a> USFX – Sucre, founded in 1624; <a href="/wiki/Universidad_Mayor_de_San_Andr%C3%A9s" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Mayor de San Andrés">Universidad Mayor de San Andrés</a> UMSA – La Paz, founded in 1830; <a href="/wiki/Universidad_Mayor_de_San_Simon" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Mayor de San Simon">Universidad Mayor de San Simon</a> UMSS – Cochabamba, founded in 1832; <a href="/wiki/Universidad_Aut%C3%B3noma_Gabriel_Ren%C3%A9_Moreno" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Autónoma Gabriel René Moreno">Universidad Autónoma Gabriel René Moreno</a> UAGRM – Santa Cruz de la Sierra, founded in 1880; <a href="/wiki/Universidad_T%C3%A9cnica_de_Oruro" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Técnica de Oruro">Universidad Técnica de Oruro</a> UTO – Oruro, founded in 1892; Universidad Evangélica Boliviana UEB – Santa Cruz de la Sierra, founded in 1980; and <a href="/wiki/Universidad_Aut%C3%B3noma_Tom%C3%A1s_Fr%C3%ADas" class="mw-redirect" title="Universidad Autónoma Tomás Frías">Universidad Autónoma Tomás Frías</a> UATF – Potosi, founded in 1892.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Bolivia" title="Health in Bolivia">Health in Bolivia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Refresh_Bolivia" title="Refresh Bolivia">Refresh Bolivia</a></div>
<p>According to UNICEF under-five mortality rate in 2006 was 52.7 per 1000 and was reduced to 26 per 1000 by 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> The infant mortality rate was 40.7 per 1000 in 2006 and was reduced to 21.2 per 1000 in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Before Morales took office, nearly half of all infants were not vaccinated; now nearly all are vaccinated. Morales also put into place several supplemental nutrition programs, including an effort to supply free food in public health and social security offices, and his desnutrición cero (zero malnutrition) program provides free school lunches.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_132-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 2006 and 2016, extreme poverty in Bolivia fell from 38.2% to 16.8%. Chronic malnutrition in children under five years of age also went down by 14% and the child mortality rate was reduced by more than 50%, according to <a href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization" title="World Health Organization">World Health Organization</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> In 2019 the Bolivian government created a universal healthcare system which has been cited as a model for all by the World Health Organization.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Women.27s_rights"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Women's_rights">Women's rights</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=44" title="Edit section: Women&#039;s rights">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>With the election of <a href="/wiki/Evo_Morales" title="Evo Morales">Evo Morales</a> to the presidency in 2005, "<a href="/wiki/Cholo" title="Cholo">chola</a>" or "cholitas" women, who had long been despised, gained new rights and social recognition. More generally, the <a href="/wiki/Bolivian_constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolivian constitution">2009 Constitution</a> improves the rights of Bolivian women.
</p><p>Despite a 2013 law against violence against women, a decade later Bolivia is the Latin American country with the highest rate of femicide.
</p><p>Thanks to a quota policy, by 2022 Bolivia will be the second country in the world, after <a href="/wiki/Rwanda" title="Rwanda">Rwanda</a>, to have as many women parliamentarians (52% in the Legislative Assembly and 47% in the Chamber of Senators).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bolivia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=45" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">Football</a> is popular. The national team is the <a href="/wiki/Bolivia_national_football_team" title="Bolivia national football team">Bolivia national football team</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Racquetball" title="Racquetball">Racquetball</a> is the second most popular sport in Bolivia as for the results in the <a href="/wiki/2018_South_American_Games" title="2018 South American Games">Odesur 2018 Games</a> held in Cochabamba.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Bolivia_at_the_Pan_American_Games" title="Bolivia at the Pan American Games">Bolivia has won 13 medals at the Pan American Games</a> and 10 of them came from racquetball events, including their only gold medal won in the <a href="/wiki/Racquetball_at_the_2019_Pan_American_Games_%E2%80%93_Men%27s_team" title="Racquetball at the 2019 Pan American Games – Men&#39;s team">Men's Team event in 2019</a>.
</p><p>Basketball is especially popular and influential in the <a href="/wiki/Potos%C3%AD_Department" title="Potosí Department">Potosí Department</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-a-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-a_1-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In Bolivia, <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Bolivia" title="Languages of Bolivia">other languages</a> have been officially recognized as legitimate <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_language" title="Indigenous language">autochthonous languages</a>.
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Quechuan_languages" title="Quechuan languages">Quechua</a>: <i>Puliwya Achka Aylluska Mamallaqta</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Aymara_language" title="Aymara language">Aymara</a>: <i lang="ay">Wuliwya Walja Suyunakana Marka</i></li></ul>
</span></li>
<li id="cite_note-central_cities-5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-central_cities_5-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-central_cities_5-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">Sucre is the constitutional capital and seat of the judicial branch of government, while the executive, legislative, and electoral branches are seated in La Paz.</span>
</li>

But political experts say the events hardly resemble a classic coup scenario. ...
